(0.289±0.101). On structurally complex ActorAttack, the advantage narrows (9-class 0.504±0.107

variance, suggesting that limited per-source volume (250 per LLM) constrains generalization to
complex attacks. DD and FITD thus primarily
validate the baseline rather than the method; ActorAttack, where lexical methods collapse (TF-IDF
F1 = 0.261) and vanilla encoders fail (F1 ≤ 0.389
across conditions), provides the discriminative evaluation axis. Mixed-LLM training confirms that
classification supervision generalizes across LLM
sources where unsupervised pretraining does not;
full results in Appendix I.

Single-LLM confound and MLM collapse.
ToxicChat (+18–42 pp; Table 5) and cross-LLM
experiments (Appendix H) confirm transfer beyond
Qwen3-8B. Mixed-LLM training reveals MLM
collapse (0.289±0.101 ≈ vanilla) while 9-class
maintains 0.818±0.096. One hypothesis: MLM
captures LLM-specific surface patterns via token
co-occurrence, while classification supervision extracts style-invariant features.

637

Variance reversal on human data. Classification supervision reduces variance 4–20× over
MLM on synthetic OOD (3 seeds); on ToxicChat
this reverses (std = 0.087 vs. 0.013). Frozen probing localizes MLM instability to Stage-2: frozen
MLM outperforms the full pipeline on ActorAttack
(0.941 vs. 0.893), while 9-class frozen matches
(0.972; Appendix R).

646

Representation and temporal analysis. CKA
confirms DAPT reshapes representations substantially, though comparable CKA shifts from differ-

ent starting points yield different detection gains—
the relationship is non-monotonic (Appendix F).
Mean pooling matches BiGRU (|∆| ≤ 1 pp; Section 4.3), confirming per-turn content features, not
sequential patterns, drive detection. Three encoders
(125M–355M) differ in architecture and pretraining; same-architecture scaling is needed to isolate
scale.

OOD benign distribution. Cross-LLM error decomposition shows true-positive rates are identical
across benign conditions; F1 degradation is driven
entirely by false positives on unfamiliar benign text
(Appendix H). On WildChat, ROC AUC = 0.700
confirms representation-level overlap that calibration cannot overcome (Appendix O). Domain adaptation amplifies benign-distribution dependence.

686

6

688

Classification-based DAPT is the most robust adaptation for base-size encoders; unsupervised pretraining closes ∼60 pp but collapses under mixedLLM training. Within the architectures tested,
larger encoders need less adaptation; 4–20× variance reduction reverses on human data. Three
negative results—label correctness, temporal order, domain specificity—constrain which properties matter. Continued pretraining is 266× faster
than LLM-as-judge (Appendix U), offering a practical alternative for multi-turn safety monitoring
given matched benign distributions (Appendix O).

703
704

Temporal aggregation dilution. When subtle
attacks (ActorAttack) are followed by extended benign padding, BiGRU temporal aggregation dilutes
attack signals (F1 drops from 0.963 to 0.383; Appendix T); in practice, adversaries would not dilute
their own successful attacks.

Conclusion

700

702

Turn-count distribution. Benign conversations
contain 12–20 turns, DD jailbreaks 6–10, and ActorAttack jailbreaks 3–8; the three categories have
non-overlapping turn counts. Three observations
argue against length as the primary detection cue:
(a) early detection at equal-length prefixes K=1
and K=2 achieves AA OOD F1 = 0.773 and 0.935
(Appendix 8); (b) vanilla models access the same
length signal but achieve near-zero OOD recall;
and (c) truncating benign conversations to 3 turns
yields FPR = 0% (Appendix T).

Lexical
separability
threshold. TF-IDF
achieves F1 = 0.961 on DD but collapses to 0.261
on ActorAttack, establishing a difficulty threshold
separating lexically solvable from structurally
complex attacks. On DD alone, beyond-lexical features cannot be attributed; the ≥63 pp ActorAttack
gap demonstrates domain-adapted representations
capture features beyond lexical overlap.

Topic variant anomaly. Topic classification
occasionally approaches MLM on ActorAttack
(0.625 vs. 0.893) despite misaligned labels, reflecting domain exposure from jailbreak text rather than
the topic objective; extreme variance (std = 0.141–
0.207) confirms initialization sensitivity.

681

the evidence hierarchy and classification supervision advantages generalize to human-authored
multi-turn attacks remains the primary open question.

9

706

OOD benign distribution. All OOD evaluations hold the benign distribution constant (Qwen38B generated); results reflect attack-side generalization. On 226 WildChat benign conversations, all variants produce FPR ≥ 44%, with 9class worst at 68.1% (Appendix O). Even with
matched benign distributions, residual calibration
error (ECE = 0.126–0.141; Appendix Q) suggests
temperature scaling is needed for deployment.

Dataset scale and cross-LLM transfer. Scale
validation (1,000 conversations; Appendix J) confirms the hierarchy holds (DAPT advantage 40–
56 pp over vanilla). Cross-LLM validation shows
jailbreak detection transfers when benign distribution is held constant (F1 recovers to 0.800–1.000),
but benign-side F1 drops to 0.370–0.430 with unfamiliar benign text (Appendix H). The perturbation
evaluation (n=76) remains underpowered.

Limitations

We plan to release annotation prompts and data for
independent verification.
Detection architecture. Binary vs. 9-class equivalence (F1 gap ≤3.9 pp) suggests the binary head
is not the critical differentiator. FITD provides minimal discriminative signal (all variants F1 ≥ 0.990),
serving as confirmation that simple attacks do not
challenge any variant.
Encoder scale. Whether findings hold at LLMscale encoders (7B+) remains open; samearchitecture scaling (e.g., DeBERTa-base vs.
DeBERTa-large) is needed to attribute the capacity
effect to scale alone.
Scope and single-turn transfer. Only
persuasion-mediated multi-turn attacks fall
within scope; single-turn token-level attacks (Zou
et al., 2023), non-persuasive strategies, and
non-text modalities are not addressed. ToxicChat
performance reflects zero-shot single-turn transfer;
doubling the training corpus drops ToxicChat
macro F1 from 0.596 to 0.341±0.004 while
multi-turn OOD remains stable, confirming the
architecture targets multi-turn detection.

784

Computation cost. Total pipeline cost for all
seven variants is under 4 GPU-hours on a single
A100.

785

Ethics Statement

786

This work is defensive: it improves detection of
multi-turn jailbreak attacks rather than developing
new attacks. We acknowledge dual-use risk, as the
persuasion taxonomy could inform adversarial actors. We mitigate this by using only synthetic conversations (no real harmful content), withholding
attack-generation prompts, and focusing contributions on detection mechanisms. No human subjects
were involved; all data will be released under CC
BY-NC 4.0.

  "old_string": "\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Topic shortcut confound.}\nThree observations argue against a topic shortcut: unstable topic OOD (F1\\,=\\,0.541$\\pm$0.207), scrambled labels matching correct within 3.9\\,pp, and label-free MLM detection (F1\\,$\\geq$\\,0.97 at $K \\leq 5$).\n\n\\paragraph{Scrambled label equivalence.}\nTwo hypotheses explain why scrambled labels match correct: (a) the classification objective regularizes representations independently of label content, or (b) with 9 classes, random permutation preserves enough class-feature correlations by chance. Mixed-LLM training reveals MLM collapse (0.289$\\pm$0.101 $\\approx$ vanilla) while 9-class maintains 0.818$\\pm$0.096. One hypothesis: MLM captures LLM-specific surface patterns via token co-occurrence, while classification supervision extracts style-invariant features.\n\n\\paragraph{Variance reversal on human data.}\nClassification supervision reduces variance 4--20$\\times$ over MLM on synthetic OOD (3 seeds); on ToxicChat this reverses (std\\,=\\,0.087 vs.\\ 0.013).\nFrozen probing localizes MLM instability to Stage-2: frozen MLM outperforms the full pipeline on ActorAttack (0.941 vs.\\ 0.893), while 9-class frozen matches (0.972; Appendix~\\ref{app:frozen_verify}).\n\n\\paragraph{Representation and temporal analysis.}\nCKA confirms DAPT reshapes representations substantially, though comparable CKA shifts from different starting points yield different detection gains---the relationship is non-monotonic (Appendix~\\ref{sec:cka_heatmap}). Mean pooling matches BiGRU ($|\\Delta| \\leq 1$\\,pp; Section~\\ref{sec:decomposition}), confirming per-turn content features, not sequential patterns, drive detection. Three encoders (125M--355M) differ in architecture and pretraining; same-architecture scaling is needed to isolate scale.\n\n\\paragraph{Lexical separability threshold.}\nTF-IDF achieves F1\\,=\\,0.961 on DD but collapses to 0.261 on ActorAttack, establishing a difficulty threshold separating lexically solvable from structurally complex attacks. On DD alone, beyond-lexical features cannot be attributed; the $\\geq$63\\,pp ActorAttack gap demonstrates domain-adapted representations capture features beyond lexical overlap.\n\n\\paragraph{OOD benign distribution.}\nCross-LLM error decomposition shows true-positive rates are identical across benign conditions; F1 degradation is driven entirely by false positives on unfamiliar benign text (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_llm}).   "new_string": "\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Topic shortcut and scrambled labels.}\nThree observations argue against a topic shortcut: unstable topic OOD (F1\\,=\\,0.541$\\pm$0.207), scrambled labels matching correct within 3.9\\,pp, and label-free MLM detection (F1\\,$\\geq$\\,0.97 at $K \\leq 5$). The scrambled-correct equivalence admits two hypotheses: classification regularizes representations independently of label content, or random permutation over 9 classes preserves enough correlations by chance; distinguishing these requires varying class count with constant domain exposure. Topic classification occasionally approaches MLM on ActorAttack (0.625 vs.\\ 0.893), reflecting domain exposure rather than the topic objective; extreme variance (std\\,=\\,0.141--0.207) confirms initialization sensitivity.\n\n\\paragraph{Single-LLM confound and MLM collapse.}\nToxicChat (+18--42\\,pp; Table~\\ref{tab:human_data}) and cross-LLM experiments (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_llm}) confirm transfer beyond Qwen3-8B. TF-IDF achieves F1\\,=\\,0.961 on DD but collapses to 0.261 on ActorAttack, establishing a difficulty threshold; the $\\geq$63\\,pp gap demonstrates beyond-lexical features.\n\n\\paragraph{OOD benign distribution.}\nCross-LLM error decomposition shows F1 degradation is driven entirely by false positives on unfamiliar benign text (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_llm}); on WildChat, ROC AUC\\,=\\,0.700 confirms representation-level overlap that calibration cannot overcome (Appendix~\\ref{app:wildchat_fpr})."

Continued pretraining is 266$\\times$ faster than LLM-as-judge (Appendix~\\ref{app:efficiency}), offering a practical alternative for multi-turn safety monitoring given matched benign distributions (Appendix~\\ref{app:wildchat_fpr}).\n\n\\section{Limitations}\n\\label{sec:limitations}\n\n\\paragraph{Multi-turn human validation.}\nAll multi-turn findings are derived from synthetic conversations generated by Qwen3-8B. ToxicChat validates transfer to human-authored single-turn attacks (+18--42\\,pp over vanilla), but no human-generated multi-turn jailbreak dataset exists for evaluation. Whether the evidence hierarchy and classification supervision advantages generalize to human-authored multi-turn attacks remains the primary open question.\n\n\\paragraph{Turn-count distribution.}\nBenign conversations contain 12--20 turns, DD jailbreaks 6--10, and ActorAttack jailbreaks 3--8; the three categories have non-overlapping turn counts. Three observations argue against length as the primary detection cue: (a) early detection at equal-length prefixes $K$=1 and $K$=2 achieves AA OOD F1\\,=\\,0.773 and 0.935 (Appendix~\\ref{tab:actorattack_full}); (b) vanilla models access the same length signal but achieve near-zero OOD recall; and (c) truncating benign conversations to 3 turns yields FPR\\,=\\,0\\% (Appendix~\\ref{app:length_independence}).\n\n\\paragraph{Temporal aggregation dilution.}\nWhen subtle attacks (ActorAttack) are followed by extended benign padding, BiGRU temporal aggregation dilutes attack signals (F1 drops from 0.963 to 0.383; Appendix~\\ref{app:length_independence}); in practice, adversaries would not dilute their own successful attacks.\n\n\\paragraph{OOD benign distribution.}\nAll OOD evaluations hold the benign distribution constant (Qwen3-8B generated); results reflect attack-side generalization. On 226 WildChat benign conversations, all variants produce FPR\\,$\\geq$\\,44\\%, with 9-class worst at 68.1\\% (Appendix~\\ref{app:wildchat_fpr}). Even with matched benign distributions, residual calibration error (ECE\\,=\\,0.126--0.141; Appendix~\\ref{app:calibration}) suggests temperature scaling is needed for deployment.\n\n\\paragraph{Frozen encoder assumption.}\nThe frozen Stage-1 design isolates representation quality but may underestimate full-pipeline performance. End-to-end fine-tuning closes this gap to $\\leq$5.8\\,pp on ActorAttack (F1\\,=\\,0.997$\\pm$0.005 vs.\\ 0.939$\\pm$0.047 frozen; Table~\\ref{tab:e2e}; Appendix~\\ref{app:frozen_verify}).\n\n\\paragraph{Dataset scale and cross-LLM transfer.}\nScale validation (1{,}000 conversations; Appendix~\\ref{app:scale_validation}) confirms the hierarchy holds (DAPT advantage 40--56\\,pp over vanilla). Cross-LLM validation shows jailbreak detection transfers when benign distribution is held constant (F1 recovers to 0.800--1.000), but benign-side F1 drops to 0.370--0.430 with unfamiliar benign text (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_llm}). The perturbation evaluation ($n$=76) remains underpowered.\n\n\\paragraph{Strategy labels.}\nPersuasion strategy labels ($n$=50 human validation, $\\kappa$=0.78) are produced by Qwen3-8B; the validation sample yields small per-class sizes across 9 categories, limiting reliability estimates for individual strategies. OOD strategy accuracy degrades to 30.7\\% ($\\kappa$=0.126); interpretability is limited to known attack families. We plan to release annotation prompts and data for independent verification.\n\n\\paragraph{Detection architecture.}\nBinary vs.\\ 9-class equivalence (F1 gap $\\leq$3.9\\,pp) suggests the binary head is not the critical differentiator. FITD provides minimal discriminative signal (all variants F1\\,$\\geq$\\,0.990), serving as confirmation that simple attacks do not challenge any variant.\n\n\\paragraph{Encoder scale.}\nWhether findings hold at LLM-scale encoders (7B+) remains open; same-architecture scaling (e.g., DeBERTa-base vs.\\ DeBERTa-large) is needed to attribute the capacity effect to scale alone.\n\n\\paragraph{Scope and single-turn transfer.}\nOnly persuasion-mediated multi-turn attacks fall within scope; single-turn token-level attacks~\\citep{zou2023gcg}, non-persuasive strategies, and non-text modalities are not addressed. ToxicChat performance reflects zero-shot single-turn transfer; doubling the training corpus drops ToxicChat macro F1 from 0.596 to 0.341$\\pm$0.004 while multi-turn OOD remains stable, confirming the architecture targets multi-turn detection.\n\n\\paragraph{Computation cost.}\nTotal pipeline cost for all seven variants is under 4 GPU-hours on a single A100.\n\n\\section*{Ethics Statement}\n\nThis work is defensive: it improves detection of multi-turn jailbreak attacks rather than developing new attacks. We acknowledge dual-use risk, as the persuasion taxonomy could inform adversarial actors. We mitigate this by using only synthetic conversations (no real harmful content), withholding attack-generation prompts, and focusing contributions on detection mechanisms. No human subjects were involved; all data will be released under CC BY-NC 4.0.\n",

          "+\\paragraph{Topic shortcut and scrambled labels.}",

The scrambled-correct equivalence admits two hypotheses: classification regularizes representations independently of label content, or random permutation over 9 classes preserves enough correlations by chance; distinguishing these requires varying class count with constant domain exposure. Mean pooling matches BiGRU ($|\\Delta| \\leq 1$\\,pp; Section~\\ref{sec:decomposition}), confirming per-turn content features drive detection; three encoders (125M--355M) differ in pretraining, so same-architecture scaling is needed. TF-IDF achieves F1\\,=\\,0.961 on DD but collapses to 0.261 on ActorAttack, establishing a difficulty threshold; the $\\geq$63\\,pp gap demonstrates beyond-lexical features.\n\n\\paragraph{OOD benign distribution.}\nCross-LLM error decomposition shows F1 degradation is driven entirely by false positives on unfamiliar benign text (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_llm}); on WildChat, ROC AUC\\,=\\,0.700 confirms representation-level overlap that calibration cannot overcome (Appendix~\\ref{app:wildchat_fpr}).",
    "newString": "\\paragraph{Representation, temporal, and lexical analysis.}\nCKA confirms DAPT reshapes representations non-monotonically (Appendix~\\ref{sec:cka_heatmap}). Mean pooling matches BiGRU ($|\\Delta| \\leq 1$\\,pp; Section~\\ref{sec:decomposition}), confirming per-turn content features drive detection. TF-IDF collapse on ActorAttack (0.261 vs.\\ 0.961 on DD) establishes a difficulty threshold; the $\\geq$63\\,pp gap demonstrates beyond-lexical features. Three encoders (125M--355M) differ in pretraining; same-architecture scaling is needed. Cross-LLM error decomposition confirms F1 degradation is driven by false positives on unfamiliar benign text, not missed attacks (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_llm}).",
    "originalFile": "% conclusion.tex — compressed for 8-page limit\n\n\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Topic shortcut and scrambled labels.}\nThree observations argue against a topic shortcut: unstable topic OOD (F1\\,=\\,0.541$\\pm$0.207), scrambled labels matching correct within 3.9\\,pp, and label-free MLM detection (F1\\,$\\geq$\\,0.97 at $K \\leq 5$). The scrambled-correct equivalence admits two hypotheses: classification regularizes representations independently of label content, or random permutation over 9 classes preserves enough correlations by chance; distinguishing these requires varying class count with constant domain exposure. Topic classification occasionally approaches MLM on ActorAttack (0.625 vs.\\ 0.893), reflecting domain exposure rather than the topic objective; extreme variance (std\\,=\\,0.141--0.207) confirms initialization sensitivity.\n\n\\paragraph{Single-LLM confound and MLM collapse.}\nToxicChat (+18--42\\,pp; Table~\\ref{tab:human_data}) and cross-LLM experiments (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_llm}) confirm transfer beyond Qwen3-8B. Mixed-LLM training reveals MLM collapse (0.289 $\\approx$ vanilla) while 9-class maintains 0.818; one hypothesis is that MLM captures LLM-specific surface patterns while classification extracts style-invariant features.\n\n\\paragraph{Variance reversal on human data.}\nClassification supervision reduces variance 4--20$\\times$ on synthetic OOD; on ToxicChat this reverses (std\\,=\\,0.087 vs.\\ 0.013). Frozen probing localizes MLM instability to Stage-2: frozen MLM outperforms the full pipeline on ActorAttack (0.941 vs.\\ 0.893), while 9-class frozen matches (0.972; Appendix~\\ref{app:frozen_verify}).\n\n\\paragraph{Representation, temporal, and lexical analysis.}\nCKA confirms DAPT reshapes representations, though comparable shifts yield different detection gains (Appendix~\\ref{sec:cka_heatmap}). Mean pooling matches BiGRU ($|\\Delta| \\leq 1$\\,pp; Section~\\ref{sec:decomposition}), confirming per-turn content features drive detection; three encoders (125M--355M) differ in pretraining, so same-architecture scaling is needed. TF-IDF achieves F1\\,=\\,0.961 on DD but collapses to 0.261 on ActorAttack, establishing a difficulty threshold; the $\\geq$63\\,pp gap demonstrates beyond-lexical features.\n\n\\paragraph{OOD benign distribution.}\nCross-LLM error decomposition shows F1 degradation is driven entirely by false positives on unfamiliar benign text (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_llm}); on WildChat, ROC AUC\\,=\\,0.700 confirms representation-level overlap that calibration cannot overcome (Appendix~\\ref{app:wildchat_fpr}).\n\n\\section{Conclusion}\n\nClassification-based DAPT is the most robust adaptation for base-size encoders; unsupervised pretraining closes $\\sim$60\\,pp but collapses under mixed-LLM training. Within the architectures tested, larger encoders need less adaptation; 4--20$\\times$ variance reduction reverses on human data. Three negative results---label correctness, temporal order, domain specificity---constrain which properties matter. Continued pretraining is 266$\\times$ faster than LLM-as-judge (Appendix~\\ref{app:efficiency}), offering a practical alternative for multi-turn safety monitoring given matched benign distributions (Appendix~\\ref{app:wildchat_fpr}).\n\n\\section{Limitations}\n\\label{sec:limitations}\n\n\\paragraph{Multi-turn human validation.}\nAll multi-turn findings are derived from synthetic conversations generated by Qwen3-8B. ToxicChat validates transfer to human-authored single-turn attacks (+18--42\\,pp over vanilla), but no human-generated multi-turn jailbreak dataset exists for evaluation. Whether the evidence hierarchy and classification supervision advantages generalize to human-authored multi-turn attacks remains the primary open question.\n\n\\paragraph{Turn-count distribution.}\nBenign conversations contain 12--20 turns, DD jailbreaks 6--10, and ActorAttack jailbreaks 3--8; the three categories have non-overlapping turn counts. Three observations argue against length as the primary detection cue: (a) early detection at equal-length prefixes $K$=1 and $K$=2 achieves AA OOD F1\\,=\\,0.773 and 0.935 (Appendix~\\ref{tab:actorattack_full}); (b) vanilla models access the same length signal but achieve near-zero OOD recall; and (c) truncating benign conversations to 3 turns yields FPR\\,=\\,0\\% (Appendix~\\ref{app:length_independence}).\n\n\\paragraph{Temporal aggregation dilution.}\nWhen subtle attacks (ActorAttack) are followed by extended benign padding, BiGRU temporal aggregation dilutes attack signals (F1 drops from 0.963 to 0.383; Appendix~\\ref{app:length_independence}); in practice, adversaries would not dilute their own successful attacks.\n\n\\paragraph{OOD benign distribution.}\nAll OOD evaluations hold the benign distribution constant (Qwen3-8B generated); results reflect attack-side generalization. On 226 WildChat benign conversations, all variants produce FPR\\,$\\geq$\\,44\\%, with 9-class worst at 68.1\\% (Appendix~\\ref{app:wildchat_fpr}). Even with matched benign distributions, residual calibration error (ECE\\,=\\,0.126--0.141; Appendix~\\ref{app:calibration}) suggests temperature scaling is needed for deployment.\n\n\\paragraph{Frozen encoder assumption.}\nThe frozen Stage-1 design isolates representation quality but may underestimate full-pipeline performance. End-to-end fine-tuning closes this gap to $\\leq$5.8\\,pp on ActorAttack (F1\\,=\\,0.997$\\pm$0.005 vs.\\ 0.939$\\pm$0.047 frozen; Table~\\ref{tab:e2e}; Appendix~\\ref{app:frozen_verify}).\n\n\\paragraph{Dataset scale and cross-LLM transfer.}\nScale validation (1{,}000 conversations; Appendix~\\ref{app:scale_validation}) confirms the hierarchy holds (DAPT advantage 40--56\\,pp over vanilla). Cross-LLM validation shows jailbreak detection transfers when benign distribution is held constant (F1 recovers to 0.800--1.000), but benign-side F1 drops to 0.370--0.430 with unfamiliar benign text (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_llm}). The perturbation evaluation ($n$=76) remains underpowered.\n\n\\paragraph{Strategy labels.}\nPersuasion strategy labels ($n$=50 human validation, $\\kappa$=0.78) are produced by Qwen3-8B; the validation sample yields small per-class sizes across 9 categories, limiting reliability estimates for individual strategies. OOD strategy accuracy degrades to 30.7\\% ($\\kappa$=0.126); interpretability is limited to known attack families. We plan to release annotation prompts and data for independent verification.\n\n\\paragraph{Detection architecture.}\nBinary vs.\\ 9-class equivalence (F1 gap $\\leq$3.9\\,pp) suggests the binary head is not the critical differentiator. FITD provides minimal discriminative signal (all variants F1\\,$\\geq$\\,0.990), serving as confirmation that simple attacks do not challenge any variant.\n\n\\paragraph{Encoder scale.}\nWhether findings hold at LLM-scale encoders (7B+) remains open; same-architecture scaling (e.g., DeBERTa-base vs.\\ DeBERTa-large) is needed to attribute the capacity effect to scale alone.\n\n\\paragraph{Scope and single-turn transfer.}\nOnly persuasion-mediated multi-turn attacks fall within scope; single-turn token-level attacks~\\citep{zou2023gcg}, non-persuasive strategies, and non-text modalities are not addressed. ToxicChat performance reflects zero-shot single-turn transfer; doubling the training corpus drops ToxicChat macro F1 from 0.596 to 0.341$\\pm$0.004 while multi-turn OOD remains stable, confirming the architecture targets multi-turn detection.\n\n\\paragraph{Computation cost.}\nTotal pipeline cost for all seven variants is under 4 GPU-hours on a single A100.\n\n\\section*{Ethics Statement}\n\nThis work is defensive: it improves detection of multi-turn jailbreak attacks rather than developing new attacks. We acknowledge dual-use risk, as the persuasion taxonomy could inform adversarial actors. We mitigate this by using only synthetic conversations (no real harmful content), withholding attack-generation prompts, and focusing contributions on detection mechanisms. No human subjects were involved; all data will be released under CC BY-NC 4.0.\n",

    "originalFile": "% introduction.tex — expanded for 8-page target\n\n\\section{Introduction}\n\nMulti-turn jailbreak attacks distribute adversarial intent across individually benign turns. Crescendo~\\citep{russinovich2024crescendo}, Foot-in-the-Door~\\citep{fitd2024}, and Deceptive Delight~\\citep{deceptivedelight2024} all rely on persuasion as their core mechanism: \\citet{zeng2024pap} catalogue 40 persuasion techniques across jailbreak families, and \\citet{hagendorff2026reasoning} identify strategies in 97\\% of successful attacks. Because individual attack turns occupy the same embedding region as benign text~\\citep{bullwinkel2025repeng}, no single-turn filter can distinguish them from legitimate conversation.\n\nExisting defenses leave a critical gap. Production classifiers such as Llama Guard~\\citep{inan2023llamaguard}, WildGuard~\\citep{han2024wildguard}, and ShieldGemma~\\citep{zeng2024shieldgemma} operate on individual turns and therefore miss manipulation that unfolds across exchanges. Multi-turn defenses have begun to model conversation-level structure: DeepContext~\\citep{deepcontext2026} pairs fine-tuned BERT with a multi-layer GRU, BIID~\\citep{tong2025biid} infers turn-level intentions, and G-Guard~\\citep{gguard2025} applies graph neural networks. These systems achieve strong in-distribution performance, but each is evaluated on a single attack family. None answers the central question: \\emph{what representation properties enable a detector to generalize across attack families it has never seen?}\n\nThe simplest hypothesis is that pretrained language understanding already suffices. It does not, at least at base model scale. A vanilla DeBERTa-v3~\\citep{he2021deberta} with temporal aggregation (cf.\\ DeepContext) drops from F1\\,=\\,1.000 in-distribution to 0.312 on out-of-distribution (OOD) Deceptive Delight. LLM-as-judge approaches fare no better: GPT-5.5 reaches only 0.191--0.449 F1 with 30--47\\% unparseable responses (Appendix~\\ref{sec:llm_judge}). These failures share a root cause: neither model has been exposed to multi-turn adversarial text. The bottleneck is distributional, not architectural: cross-attack generalization requires domain adaptation, not a better classifier.\n\nWe test this hypothesis through controlled ablation. Our pipeline, \\textbf{DisPeD} (Disentangled Persuasion Detection), separates encoder adaptation (Stage~1) from temporal classification (Stage~2, a frozen BiGRU; Figure~\\ref{fig:architecture}). Seven Stage~1 variants each test a single factor (e.g., domain exposure, label quality, temporal modeling) while holding all other components constant (Section~\\ref{sec:method}). The contribution is the systematic decomposition of what enables cross-attack generalization, not the pipeline itself.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/paper/fig1_architecture.pdf}\n\\caption{DisPeD architecture. Stage~1 adapts a DeBERTa encoder on jailbreak conversation text (seven variant objectives). Stage~2 aggregates frozen \\texttt{[CLS]} embeddings via BiGRU for conversation-level jailbreak detection. Only user turns are processed.}\n\\label{fig:architecture}\n\\end{figure}\n\nControlled ablation across DeBERTa-v3 and RoBERTa~\\citep{liu2019roberta} on four attack families and two training compositions shows that for base-size encoders (125--184M), classification-based adaptation is the most robust variant across synthetic multi-turn OOD attacks. Continued pretraining closes $\\sim$60\\,pp on single-source data but MLM collapses on mixed-LLM training (F1\\,=\\,0.289 $\\approx$ vanilla), while classification DAPT maintains 0.818. This dependence diminishes with scale: RoBERTa-large (355M) handles even ActorAttack~\\citep{ren2024actorattack} without adaptation. A variance reduction of 4--20$\\times$ is observed (3 seeds) for classification supervision on synthetic data but not on human-authored conversations (Section~\\ref{sec:human_data}).\n\nOur contributions:\n\\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*, itemsep=2pt, topsep=4pt]\n  \\item \\textbf{Systematic decomposition of cross-attack generalization.} Controlled ablation across thirteen encoder configurations, three OOD attack families, and two training compositions on synthetic multi-turn data identifies classification-based domain adaptation as the most robust variant; unsupervised pretraining is effective on single-source data but collapses on mixed-LLM training. Three negative results are counter-intuitive: scrambled labels match correct labels (equivalence confirmed on DD via TOST at $\\delta$=5\\,pp, underpowered on ActorAttack), temporal order is irrelevant, and label granularity does not affect detection.\n\n  \\item \\textbf{Scale-dependent observation on domain adaptation.} Three encoder scales (125M, 184M, 355M) suggest that the set of attacks requiring adaptation shrinks with model size; however, encoders differ in architecture and pretraining, so same-architecture scaling is needed to isolate size as the causal factor. RoBERTa-large handles structurally complex attacks without adaptation (Section~\\ref{sec:cross_encoder}).\n\n  \\item \\textbf{Condition-dependent adaptation effects.} A variance reduction of 4--20$\\times$ is observed (3 seeds) on synthetic data, but this benefit reverses on human-authored data. Mixed-LLM training validates that classification-based DAPT remains the strongest variant across training compositions, though the magnitude of advantage varies by attack complexity (Section~\\ref{sec:discussion}). The 9-class taxonomy serves as both an interpretability tool for characterizing known attacks and ablation infrastructure for testing whether label quality affects detection (Section~\\ref{sec:strategy}).\n\\end{itemize}\n",

Only user turns are processed.}\n\\label{fig:architecture}\n\\end{figure}\n\nControlled ablation across DeBERTa-v3 and RoBERTa~\\citep{liu2019roberta} on four attack families and two training compositions shows that for base-size encoders (125--184M), classification-based adaptation is the most robust variant across synthetic multi-turn OOD attacks. Continued pretraining closes $\\sim$60\\,pp on single-source data but MLM collapses on mixed-LLM training (F1\\,=\\,0.289 $\\approx$ vanilla), while classification DAPT maintains 0.818. This dependence diminishes with scale: RoBERTa-large (355M) handles even ActorAttack~\\citep{ren2024actorattack} without adaptation. A variance reduction of 4--20$\\times$ is observed (3 seeds) for classification supervision on synthetic data but not on human-authored conversations (Section~\\ref{sec:human_data}).\n\nOur contributions:\n\\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*, itemsep=2pt, topsep=4pt]\n  \\item \\textbf{Systematic decomposition of cross-attack generalization.} Controlled ablation across thirteen encoder configurations, three OOD attack families, and two training compositions on synthetic multi-turn data identifies classification-based domain adaptation as the most robust variant; unsupervised pretraining is effective on single-source data but collapses on mixed-LLM training. Three negative results are counter-intuitive: scrambled labels match correct labels (TOST-confirmed on DD; underpowered on ActorAttack), temporal order is irrelevant, and label granularity does not affect detection.\n\n  \\item \\textbf{Scale-dependent observation on domain adaptation.} Three encoder scales (125M, 184M, 355M) suggest adaptation need shrinks with model size, though encoders differ in architecture; same-architecture scaling is needed to isolate scale. RoBERTa-large handles structurally complex attacks without adaptation (Section~\\ref{sec:cross_encoder}).\n\n  \\item \\textbf{Condition-dependent adaptation effects.} A variance reduction of 4--20$\\times$ is observed (3 seeds) on synthetic data, but this benefit reverses on human-authored data. Mixed-LLM training validates that classification-based DAPT remains the strongest variant across training compositions, though the magnitude of advantage varies by attack complexity (Section~\\ref{sec:discussion}). The 9-class taxonomy serves as both an interpretability tool for characterizing known attacks and ablation infrastructure for testing whether label quality affects detection (Section~\\ref{sec:strategy}).\n\\end{itemize}\n",

    "originalFile": "% introduction.tex — expanded for 8-page target\n\n\\section{Introduction}\n\nMulti-turn jailbreak attacks distribute adversarial intent across individually benign turns. Crescendo~\\citep{russinovich2024crescendo}, Foot-in-the-Door~\\citep{fitd2024}, and Deceptive Delight~\\citep{deceptivedelight2024} all rely on persuasion as their core mechanism: \\citet{zeng2024pap} catalogue 40 persuasion techniques across jailbreak families, and \\citet{hagendorff2026reasoning} identify strategies in 97\\% of successful attacks. Because individual attack turns occupy the same embedding region as benign text~\\citep{bullwinkel2025repeng}, no single-turn filter can distinguish them from legitimate conversation.\n\nExisting defenses leave a critical gap. Production classifiers such as Llama Guard~\\citep{inan2023llamaguard}, WildGuard~\\citep{han2024wildguard}, and ShieldGemma~\\citep{zeng2024shieldgemma} operate on individual turns and therefore miss manipulation that unfolds across exchanges. Multi-turn defenses have begun to model conversation-level structure: DeepContext~\\citep{deepcontext2026} pairs fine-tuned BERT with a multi-layer GRU, BIID~\\citep{tong2025biid} infers turn-level intentions, and G-Guard~\\citep{gguard2025} applies graph neural networks. These systems achieve strong in-distribution performance, but each is evaluated on a single attack family. None answers the central question: \\emph{what representation properties enable a detector to generalize across attack families it has never seen?}\n\nThe simplest hypothesis is that pretrained language understanding already suffices. It does not, at least at base model scale. A vanilla DeBERTa-v3~\\citep{he2021deberta} with temporal aggregation (cf.\\ DeepContext) drops from F1\\,=\\,1.000 in-distribution to 0.312 on out-of-distribution (OOD) Deceptive Delight. LLM-as-judge approaches fare no better: GPT-5.5 reaches only 0.191--0.449 F1 with 30--47\\% unparseable responses (Appendix~\\ref{sec:llm_judge}). These failures share a root cause: neither model has been exposed to multi-turn adversarial text. The bottleneck is distributional, not architectural: cross-attack generalization requires domain adaptation, not a better classifier.\n\nWe test this hypothesis through controlled ablation. Our pipeline, \\textbf{DisPeD} (Disentangled Persuasion Detection), separates encoder adaptation (Stage~1) from temporal classification (Stage~2, a frozen BiGRU; Figure~\\ref{fig:architecture}). Seven Stage~1 variants each test a single factor (e.g., domain exposure, label quality, temporal modeling) while holding all other components constant (Section~\\ref{sec:method}). The contribution is the systematic decomposition of what enables cross-attack generalization, not the pipeline itself.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/paper/fig1_architecture.pdf}\n\\caption{DisPeD architecture. Continued pretraining closes $\\sim$60\\,pp on single-source data but MLM collapses on mixed-LLM training (F1\\,=\\,0.289 $\\approx$ vanilla), while classification DAPT maintains 0.818. This dependence diminishes with scale: RoBERTa-large (355M) handles even ActorAttack~\\citep{ren2024actorattack} without adaptation. Three negative results are counter-intuitive: scrambled labels match correct labels (TOST-confirmed on DD; underpowered on ActorAttack), temporal order is irrelevant, and label granularity does not affect detection.\n\n  \\item \\textbf{Scale-dependent observation on domain adaptation.} Three encoder scales (125M, 184M, 355M) suggest that the set of attacks requiring adaptation shrinks with model size; however, encoders differ in architecture and pretraining, so same-architecture scaling is needed to isolate size as the causal factor.   "old_string": "\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Topic shortcut and scrambled labels.}\nThree observations argue against a topic shortcut: unstable topic OOD (F1\\,=\\,0.541$\\pm$0.207), scrambled labels matching correct within 3.9\\,pp, and label-free MLM detection (F1\\,$\\geq$\\,0.97 at $K \\leq 5$). The scrambled-correct equivalence admits two hypotheses: classification regularizes representations independently of label content, or random permutation over 9 classes preserves enough correlations by chance; distinguishing these requires varying class count with constant domain exposure. Topic classification occasionally approaches MLM on ActorAttack (0.625 vs.\\ 0.893), reflecting domain exposure rather than the topic objective; extreme variance (std\\,=\\,0.141--0.207) confirms initialization sensitivity.\n\n\\paragraph{Single-LLM confound and MLM collapse.}\nToxicChat (+18--42\\,pp; Table~\\ref{tab:human_data}) and cross-LLM experiments (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_llm}) confirm transfer beyond Qwen3-8B. Mixed-LLM training reveals MLM collapse (0.289 $\\approx$ vanilla) while 9-class maintains 0.818; one hypothesis is that MLM captures LLM-specific surface patterns while classification extracts style-invariant features.\n\n\\paragraph{Variance reversal on human data.}\nClassification supervision reduces variance 4--20$\\times$ on synthetic OOD; on ToxicChat this reverses (std\\,=\\,0.087 vs.\\ 0.013). Frozen probing localizes MLM instability to Stage-2: frozen MLM outperforms the full pipeline on ActorAttack (0.941 vs.\\ 0.893), while 9-class frozen matches (0.972; Appendix~\\ref{app:frozen_verify}).\n\n\\paragraph{Representation, temporal, and lexical analysis.}\nCKA confirms DAPT reshapes representations non-monotonically (Appendix~\\ref{sec:cka_heatmap}). Mean pooling matches BiGRU ($|\\Delta| \\leq 1$\\,pp; Section~\\ref{sec:decomposition}), confirming per-turn content features drive detection. TF-IDF collapse on ActorAttack (0.261 vs.\\ 0.961 on DD) establishes a difficulty threshold; the $\\geq$63\\,pp gap demonstrates beyond-lexical features. Three encoders (125M--355M) differ in pretraining; same-architecture scaling is needed. Cross-LLM error decomposition confirms F1 degradation is driven by false positives on unfamiliar benign text, not missed attacks (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_llm}).",
  "new_string": "\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Topic shortcut and label equivalence.}\nThree observations argue against a topic shortcut: unstable topic OOD (F1\\,=\\,0.541$\\pm$0.207), scrambled labels matching correct within 3.9\\,pp, and label-free MLM detection (F1\\,$\\geq$\\,0.97 at $K \\leq 5$). The scrambled-correct equivalence could reflect classification regularizing representations independently of label content, or random permutation over 9 classes preserving enough correlations; distinguishing these requires varying class count. Topic classification occasionally approaches MLM on ActorAttack (0.625 vs.\\ 0.893) despite misaligned labels, reflecting domain exposure rather than the topic objective.\n\n\\paragraph{Single-LLM confound.}\nToxicChat (+18--42\\,pp; Table~\\ref{tab:human_data}) and cross-LLM experiments (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_llm}) confirm transfer. Mixed-LLM 9-class maintains DD F1\\,=\\,0.818$\\pm$0.096 (+53\\,pp) while MLM collapses; one hypothesis is that MLM captures LLM-specific surface patterns while classification extracts style-invariant features. Cross-LLM error decomposition confirms F1 degradation is driven by false positives on unfamiliar benign text, not missed attacks.\n\n\\paragraph{Variance reversal on human data.}\nClassification supervision reduces variance 4--20$\\times$ over MLM on synthetic OOD (3 seeds); on ToxicChat this reverses (std\\,=\\,0.087 vs.\\ 0.013).\nFrozen probing localizes MLM instability to Stage-2: frozen MLM outperforms the full pipeline on ActorAttack (0.941 vs.\\ 0.893), while 9-class frozen matches (0.972; Appendix~\\ref{app:frozen_verify}).\n\n\\paragraph{Encoder capacity, temporal, and lexical analysis.}\nThree encoders (125M--355M) differ in pretraining; same-architecture scaling is needed. Mean pooling matches BiGRU and shuffling produces $|\\Delta| < 1$\\,pp (Section~\\ref{sec:decomposition}), confirming order-invariant detection. CKA confirms DAPT reshapes representations non-monotonically (Appendix~\\ref{sec:cka_heatmap}). TF-IDF collapse on ActorAttack (0.261 vs.\\ 0.961 on DD) demonstrates beyond-lexical features (Section~\\ref{sec:limitations})."
}

    "newString": "\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Topic shortcut and label equivalence.}\nThree observations argue against a topic shortcut: unstable topic OOD (F1\\,=\\,0.541$\\pm$0.207), scrambled labels matching correct within 3.9\\,pp, and label-free MLM detection (F1\\,$\\geq$\\,0.97 at $K \\leq 5$).     "originalFile": "% conclusion.tex — compressed for 8-page limit\n\n\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Topic shortcut and scrambled labels.}\nThree observations argue against a topic shortcut: unstable topic OOD (F1\\,=\\,0.541$\\pm$0.207), scrambled labels matching correct within 3.9\\,pp, and label-free MLM detection (F1\\,$\\geq$\\,0.97 at $K \\leq 5$). The scrambled-correct equivalence admits two hypotheses: classification regularizes representations independently of label content, or random permutation over 9 classes preserves enough correlations by chance; distinguishing these requires varying class count with constant domain exposure. Topic classification occasionally approaches MLM on ActorAttack (0.625 vs.\\ 0.893), reflecting domain exposure rather than the topic objective; extreme variance (std\\,=\\,0.141--0.207) confirms initialization sensitivity.\n\n\\paragraph{Single-LLM confound and MLM collapse.}\nToxicChat (+18--42\\,pp; Table~\\ref{tab:human_data}) and cross-LLM experiments (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_llm}) confirm transfer beyond Qwen3-8B. Mixed-LLM training reveals MLM collapse (0.289 $\\approx$ vanilla) while 9-class maintains 0.818; one hypothesis is that MLM captures LLM-specific surface patterns while classification extracts style-invariant features.\n\n\\paragraph{Variance reversal on human data.}\nClassification supervision reduces variance 4--20$\\times$ on synthetic OOD; on ToxicChat this reverses (std\\,=\\,0.087 vs.\\ 0.013). Frozen probing localizes MLM instability to Stage-2: frozen MLM outperforms the full pipeline on ActorAttack (0.941 vs.\\ 0.893), while 9-class frozen matches (0.972; Appendix~\\ref{app:frozen_verify}).\n\n\\paragraph{Representation, temporal, and lexical analysis.}\nCKA confirms DAPT reshapes representations non-monotonically (Appendix~\\ref{sec:cka_heatmap}). Mean pooling matches BiGRU ($|\\Delta| \\leq 1$\\,pp; Section~\\ref{sec:decomposition}), confirming per-turn content features drive detection. TF-IDF collapse on ActorAttack (0.261 vs.\\ 0.961 on DD) establishes a difficulty threshold; the $\\geq$63\\,pp gap demonstrates beyond-lexical features. Three encoders (125M--355M) differ in pretraining; same-architecture scaling is needed. Cross-LLM error decomposition confirms F1 degradation is driven by false positives on unfamiliar benign text, not missed attacks (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_llm}).\n\n\\section{Conclusion}\n\nClassification-based DAPT is the most robust adaptation for base-size encoders; unsupervised pretraining closes $\\sim$60\\,pp but collapses under mixed-LLM training. Within the architectures tested, larger encoders need less adaptation; 4--20$\\times$ variance reduction reverses on human data. Three negative results---label correctness, temporal order, domain specificity---constrain which properties matter. Continued pretraining is 266$\\times$ faster than LLM-as-judge (Appendix~\\ref{app:efficiency}), offering a practical alternative for multi-turn safety monitoring given matched benign distributions (Appendix~\\ref{app:wildchat_fpr}).\n\n\\section{Limitations}\n\\label{sec:limitations}\n\n\\paragraph{Multi-turn human validation.}\nAll multi-turn findings are derived from synthetic conversations generated by Qwen3-8B. ToxicChat validates transfer to human-authored single-turn attacks (+18--42\\,pp over vanilla), but no human-generated multi-turn jailbreak dataset exists for evaluation. Whether the evidence hierarchy and classification supervision advantages generalize to human-authored multi-turn attacks remains the primary open question.\n\n\\paragraph{Turn-count distribution.}\nBenign conversations contain 12--20 turns, DD jailbreaks 6--10, and ActorAttack jailbreaks 3--8; the three categories have non-overlapping turn counts. Three observations argue against length as the primary detection cue: (a) early detection at equal-length prefixes $K$=1 and $K$=2 achieves AA OOD F1\\,=\\,0.773 and 0.935 (Appendix~\\ref{tab:actorattack_full}); (b) vanilla models access the same length signal but achieve near-zero OOD recall; and (c) truncating benign conversations to 3 turns yields FPR\\,=\\,0\\% (Appendix~\\ref{app:length_independence}).\n\n\\paragraph{Temporal aggregation dilution.}\nWhen subtle attacks (ActorAttack) are followed by extended benign padding, BiGRU temporal aggregation dilutes attack signals (F1 drops from 0.963 to 0.383; Appendix~\\ref{app:length_independence}); in practice, adversaries would not dilute their own successful attacks.\n\n\\paragraph{OOD benign distribution.}\nAll OOD evaluations hold the benign distribution constant (Qwen3-8B generated); results reflect attack-side generalization. On 226 WildChat benign conversations, all variants produce FPR\\,$\\geq$\\,44\\%, with 9-class worst at 68.1\\% (Appendix~\\ref{app:wildchat_fpr}). Even with matched benign distributions, residual calibration error (ECE\\,=\\,0.126--0.141; Appendix~\\ref{app:calibration}) suggests temperature scaling is needed for deployment.\n\n\\paragraph{Frozen encoder assumption.}\nThe frozen Stage-1 design isolates representation quality but may underestimate full-pipeline performance. End-to-end fine-tuning closes this gap to $\\leq$5.8\\,pp on ActorAttack (F1\\,=\\,0.997$\\pm$0.005 vs.\\ 0.939$\\pm$0.047 frozen; Table~\\ref{tab:e2e}; Appendix~\\ref{app:frozen_verify}).\n\n\\paragraph{Dataset scale and cross-LLM transfer.}\nScale validation (1{,}000 conversations; Appendix~\\ref{app:scale_validation}) confirms the hierarchy holds (DAPT advantage 40--56\\,pp over vanilla). Cross-LLM validation shows jailbreak detection transfers when benign distribution is held constant (F1 recovers to 0.800--1.000), but benign-side F1 drops to 0.370--0.430 with unfamiliar benign text (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_llm}). The perturbation evaluation ($n$=76) remains underpowered.\n\n\\paragraph{Strategy labels.}\nPersuasion strategy labels ($n$=50 human validation, $\\kappa$=0.78) are produced by Qwen3-8B; the validation sample yields small per-class sizes across 9 categories, limiting reliability estimates for individual strategies. OOD strategy accuracy degrades to 30.7\\% ($\\kappa$=0.126); interpretability is limited to known attack families. We plan to release annotation prompts and data for independent verification.\n\n\\paragraph{Detection architecture.}\nBinary vs.\\ 9-class equivalence (F1 gap $\\leq$3.9\\,pp) suggests the binary head is not the critical differentiator. FITD provides minimal discriminative signal (all variants F1\\,$\\geq$\\,0.990), serving as confirmation that simple attacks do not challenge any variant.\n\n\\paragraph{Encoder scale.}\nWhether findings hold at LLM-scale encoders (7B+) remains open; same-architecture scaling (e.g., DeBERTa-base vs.\\ DeBERTa-large) is needed to attribute the capacity effect to scale alone.\n\n\\paragraph{Scope and single-turn transfer.}\nOnly persuasion-mediated multi-turn attacks fall within scope; single-turn token-level attacks~\\citep{zou2023gcg}, non-persuasive strategies, and non-text modalities are not addressed. ToxicChat performance reflects zero-shot single-turn transfer; doubling the training corpus drops ToxicChat macro F1 from 0.596 to 0.341$\\pm$0.004 while multi-turn OOD remains stable, confirming the architecture targets multi-turn detection.\n\n\\paragraph{Computation cost.}\nTotal pipeline cost for all seven variants is under 4 GPU-hours on a single A100.\n\n\\section*{Ethics Statement}\n\nThis work is defensive: it improves detection of multi-turn jailbreak attacks rather than developing new attacks. We acknowledge dual-use risk, as the persuasion taxonomy could inform adversarial actors. We mitigate this by using only synthetic conversations (no real harmful content), withholding attack-generation prompts, and focusing contributions on detection mechanisms. No human subjects were involved; all data will be released under CC BY-NC 4.0.\n",

    "oldString": "Controlled ablation across DeBERTa-v3 and RoBERTa~\\citep{liu2019roberta} on four attack families and two training compositions shows that for base-size encoders (125--184M), classification-based adaptation is the most robust variant across synthetic multi-turn OOD attacks. Continued pretraining closes $\\sim$60\\,pp on single-source data but MLM collapses on mixed-LLM training (F1\\,=\\,0.289 $\\approx$ vanilla), while classification DAPT maintains 0.818. This dependence diminishes with scale: RoBERTa-large (355M) handles even ActorAttack~\\citep{ren2024actorattack} without adaptation. A variance reduction of 4--20$\\times$ is observed (3 seeds) for classification supervision on synthetic data but not on human-authored conversations (Section~\\ref{sec:human_data}).",
    "newString": "Controlled ablation across DeBERTa-v3 and RoBERTa~\\citep{liu2019roberta} on four attack families and two training compositions shows that for base-size encoders, classification-based adaptation is the most robust variant on synthetic multi-turn OOD attacks. MLM collapses on mixed-LLM training (F1\\,=\\,0.289 $\\approx$ vanilla) while classification DAPT maintains 0.818; RoBERTa-large (355M) handles ActorAttack~\\citep{ren2024actorattack} without adaptation. A 4--20$\\times$ variance reduction is observed for classification supervision on synthetic data but not on human-authored conversations (Section~\\ref{sec:human_data}).",
    "originalFile": "% introduction.tex — expanded for 8-page target\n\n\\section{Introduction}\n\nMulti-turn jailbreak attacks distribute adversarial intent across individually benign turns. Crescendo~\\citep{russinovich2024crescendo}, Foot-in-the-Door~\\citep{fitd2024}, and Deceptive Delight~\\citep{deceptivedelight2024} all rely on persuasion as their core mechanism: \\citet{zeng2024pap} catalogue 40 persuasion techniques across jailbreak families, and \\citet{hagendorff2026reasoning} identify strategies in 97\\% of successful attacks. Because individual attack turns occupy the same embedding region as benign text~\\citep{bullwinkel2025repeng}, no single-turn filter can distinguish them from legitimate conversation.\n\nExisting defenses leave a critical gap. Production classifiers such as Llama Guard~\\citep{inan2023llamaguard}, WildGuard~\\citep{han2024wildguard}, and ShieldGemma~\\citep{zeng2024shieldgemma} operate on individual turns and therefore miss manipulation that unfolds across exchanges. Multi-turn defenses have begun to model conversation-level structure: DeepContext~\\citep{deepcontext2026} pairs fine-tuned BERT with a multi-layer GRU, BIID~\\citep{tong2025biid} infers turn-level intentions, and G-Guard~\\citep{gguard2025} applies graph neural networks. These systems achieve strong in-distribution performance, but each is evaluated on a single attack family. None answers the central question: \\emph{what representation properties enable a detector to generalize across attack families it has never seen?}\n\nPretrained language understanding does not suffice at base model scale: vanilla DeBERTa-v3~\\citep{he2021deberta} with temporal aggregation (cf.\\ DeepContext) drops from F1\\,=\\,1.000 in-distribution to 0.312 on OOD Deceptive Delight, and GPT-5.5 reaches only 0.191--0.449 F1 with 30--47\\% unparseable responses (Appendix~\\ref{sec:llm_judge}). The bottleneck is distributional: cross-attack generalization requires domain adaptation, not a better classifier.\n\nWe test this hypothesis through controlled ablation. Our pipeline, \\textbf{DisPeD} (Disentangled Persuasion Detection), separates encoder adaptation (Stage~1) from temporal classification (Stage~2, a frozen BiGRU; Figure~\\ref{fig:architecture}). Seven Stage~1 variants each test a single factor (e.g., domain exposure, label quality, temporal modeling) while holding all other components constant (Section~\\ref{sec:method}). The contribution is the systematic decomposition of what enables cross-attack generalization, not the pipeline itself.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/paper/fig1_architecture.pdf}\n\\caption{DisPeD architecture. Stage~1 adapts a DeBERTa encoder on jailbreak conversation text (seven variant objectives). Stage~2 aggregates frozen \\texttt{[CLS]} embeddings via BiGRU for conversation-level jailbreak detection. Only user turns are processed.}\n\\label{fig:architecture}\n\\end{figure}\n\nControlled ablation across DeBERTa-v3 and RoBERTa~\\citep{liu2019roberta} on four attack families and two training compositions shows that for base-size encoders (125--184M), classification-based adaptation is the most robust variant across synthetic multi-turn OOD attacks. Continued pretraining closes $\\sim$60\\,pp on single-source data but MLM collapses on mixed-LLM training (F1\\,=\\,0.289 $\\approx$ vanilla), while classification DAPT maintains 0.818. This dependence diminishes with scale: RoBERTa-large (355M) handles even ActorAttack~\\citep{ren2024actorattack} without adaptation. A variance reduction of 4--20$\\times$ is observed (3 seeds) for classification supervision on synthetic data but not on human-authored conversations (Section~\\ref{sec:human_data}).\n\nOur contributions:\n\\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*, itemsep=2pt, topsep=4pt]\n  \\item \\textbf{Systematic decomposition of cross-attack generalization.} Controlled ablation across thirteen encoder configurations, three OOD attack families, and two training compositions on synthetic multi-turn data identifies classification-based domain adaptation as the most robust variant; unsupervised pretraining is effective on single-source data but collapses on mixed-LLM training. Three negative results are counter-intuitive: scrambled labels match correct labels (TOST-confirmed on DD; underpowered on ActorAttack), temporal order is irrelevant, and label granularity does not affect detection.\n\n  \\item \\textbf{Scale-dependent observation on domain adaptation.} Three encoder scales (125M, 184M, 355M) suggest adaptation need shrinks with model size, though encoders differ in architecture; same-architecture scaling is needed to isolate scale. RoBERTa-large handles structurally complex attacks without adaptation (Section~\\ref{sec:cross_encoder}).\n\n  \\item \\textbf{Condition-dependent adaptation effects.} A variance reduction of 4--20$\\times$ is observed (3 seeds) on synthetic data, but this benefit reverses on human-authored data. Mixed-LLM training validates that classification-based DAPT remains the strongest variant across training compositions, though the magnitude of advantage varies by attack complexity (Section~\\ref{sec:discussion}). The 9-class taxonomy serves as interpretability tool and ablation infrastructure for label quality assessment (Section~\\ref{sec:strategy}).\n\\end{itemize}\n",

    "originalFile": "% introduction.tex — expanded for 8-page target\n\n\\section{Introduction}\n\nMulti-turn jailbreak attacks distribute adversarial intent across individually benign turns. Crescendo~\\citep{russinovich2024crescendo}, Foot-in-the-Door~\\citep{fitd2024}, and Deceptive Delight~\\citep{deceptivedelight2024} all rely on persuasion as their core mechanism: \\citet{zeng2024pap} catalogue 40 persuasion techniques across jailbreak families, and \\citet{hagendorff2026reasoning} identify strategies in 97\\% of successful attacks. Because individual attack turns occupy the same embedding region as benign text~\\citep{bullwinkel2025repeng}, no single-turn filter can distinguish them from legitimate conversation.\n\nExisting defenses leave a critical gap. Production classifiers such as Llama Guard~\\citep{inan2023llamaguard}, WildGuard~\\citep{han2024wildguard}, and ShieldGemma~\\citep{zeng2024shieldgemma} operate on individual turns and therefore miss manipulation that unfolds across exchanges. Multi-turn defenses have begun to model conversation-level structure: DeepContext~\\citep{deepcontext2026} pairs fine-tuned BERT with a multi-layer GRU, BIID~\\citep{tong2025biid} infers turn-level intentions, and G-Guard~\\citep{gguard2025} applies graph neural networks. These systems achieve strong in-distribution performance, but each is evaluated on a single attack family. None answers the central question: \\emph{what representation properties enable a detector to generalize across attack families it has never seen?}\n\nThe simplest hypothesis is that pretrained language understanding already suffices. It does not, at least at base model scale. A vanilla DeBERTa-v3~\\citep{he2021deberta} with temporal aggregation (cf.\\ DeepContext) drops from F1\\,=\\,1.000 in-distribution to 0.312 on out-of-distribution (OOD) Deceptive Delight. LLM-as-judge approaches fare no better: GPT-5.5 reaches only 0.191--0.449 F1 with 30--47\\% unparseable responses (Appendix~\\ref{sec:llm_judge}). These failures share a root cause: neither model has been exposed to multi-turn adversarial text. The bottleneck is distributional, not architectural: cross-attack generalization requires domain adaptation, not a better classifier.\n\nWe test this hypothesis through controlled ablation. Our pipeline, \\textbf{DisPeD} (Disentangled Persuasion Detection), separates encoder adaptation (Stage~1) from temporal classification (Stage~2, a frozen BiGRU; Figure~\\ref{fig:architecture}). Seven Stage~1 variants each test a single factor (e.g., domain exposure, label quality, temporal modeling) while holding all other components constant (Section~\\ref{sec:method}). 9	Pretrained language understanding does not suffice at base model scale: vanilla DeBERTa-v3~\citep{he2021deberta} with temporal aggregation (cf.\ DeepContext) drops from F1\,=\,1.000 in-distribution to 0.312 on OOD Deceptive Delight, and GPT-5.5 reaches only 0.191--0.449 F1 with 30--47\% unparseable responses (Appendix~\ref{sec:llm_judge}). The bottleneck is distributional: cross-attack generalization requires domain adaptation, not a better classifier.
10	
11	We test this hypothesis through controlled ablation. Our pipeline, \textbf{DisPeD} (Disentangled Persuasion Detection), separates encoder adaptation (Stage~1) from temporal classification (Stage~2, a frozen BiGRU; Figure~\ref{fig:architecture}). Seven Stage~1 variants each test a single factor (e.g., domain exposure, label quality, temporal modeling) while holding all other components constant (Section~\ref{sec:method}). The contribution is the systematic decomposition of what enables cross-attack generalization, not the pipeline itself.
12	
13	\begin{figure}[t]
```

Seven Stage~1 variants each test a single factor (e.g., domain exposure, label quality, temporal modeling) while holding all other components constant (Section~\\ref{sec:method}). The contribution is the systematic decomposition of what enables cross-attack generalization, not the pipeline itself.",
    "newString": "Our pipeline, \\textbf{DisPeD} (Disentangled Persuasion Detection), separates encoder adaptation (Stage~1) from temporal classification (Stage~2, a frozen BiGRU; Figure~\\ref{fig:architecture}). Seven Stage~1 variants each isolate a single factor while holding all others constant (Section~\\ref{sec:method}); the contribution is the decomposition, not the pipeline.",
    "originalFile": "% introduction.tex — expanded for 8-page target\n\n\\section{Introduction}\n\nMulti-turn jailbreak attacks distribute adversarial intent across individually benign turns. Crescendo~\\citep{russinovich2024crescendo}, Foot-in-the-Door~\\citep{fitd2024}, and Deceptive Delight~\\citep{deceptivedelight2024} all rely on persuasion as their core mechanism: \\citet{zeng2024pap} catalogue 40 persuasion techniques across jailbreak families, and \\citet{hagendorff2026reasoning} identify strategies in 97\\% of successful attacks. Because individual attack turns occupy the same embedding region as benign text~\\citep{bullwinkel2025repeng}, no single-turn filter can distinguish them from legitimate conversation.\n\nExisting defenses leave a critical gap. Production classifiers such as Llama Guard~\\citep{inan2023llamaguard}, WildGuard~\\citep{han2024wildguard}, and ShieldGemma~\\citep{zeng2024shieldgemma} operate on individual turns and therefore miss manipulation that unfolds across exchanges. Multi-turn defenses have begun to model conversation-level structure: DeepContext~\\citep{deepcontext2026} pairs fine-tuned BERT with a multi-layer GRU, BIID~\\citep{tong2025biid} infers turn-level intentions, and G-Guard~\\citep{gguard2025} applies graph neural networks. These systems achieve strong in-distribution performance, but each is evaluated on a single attack family. None answers the central question: \\emph{what representation properties enable a detector to generalize across attack families it has never seen?}\n\nPretrained language understanding does not suffice at base model scale: vanilla DeBERTa-v3~\\citep{he2021deberta} with temporal aggregation (cf.\\ DeepContext) drops from F1\\,=\\,1.000 in-distribution to 0.312 on OOD Deceptive Delight, and GPT-5.5 reaches only 0.191--0.449 F1 with 30--47\\% unparseable responses (Appendix~\\ref{sec:llm_judge}). The bottleneck is distributional: cross-attack generalization requires domain adaptation, not a better classifier.\n\nWe test this hypothesis through controlled ablation. Our pipeline, \\textbf{DisPeD} (Disentangled Persuasion Detection), separates encoder adaptation (Stage~1) from temporal classification (Stage~2, a frozen BiGRU; Figure~\\ref{fig:architecture}). Seven Stage~1 variants each test a single factor (e.g., domain exposure, label quality, temporal modeling) while holding all other components constant (Section~\\ref{sec:method}). The contribution is the systematic decomposition of what enables cross-attack generalization, not the pipeline itself.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/paper/fig1_architecture.pdf}\n\\caption{DisPeD architecture. Stage~1 adapts a DeBERTa encoder on jailbreak conversation text (seven variant objectives). Stage~2 aggregates frozen \\texttt{[CLS]} embeddings via BiGRU for conversation-level jailbreak detection. Only user turns are processed.}\n\\label{fig:architecture}\n\\end{figure}\n\nControlled ablation across DeBERTa-v3 and RoBERTa~\\citep{liu2019roberta} on four attack families and two training compositions shows that for base-size encoders, classification-based adaptation is the most robust variant on synthetic multi-turn OOD attacks. MLM collapses on mixed-LLM training (F1\\,=\\,0.289 $\\approx$ vanilla) while classification DAPT maintains 0.818; RoBERTa-large (355M) handles ActorAttack~\\citep{ren2024actorattack} without adaptation. A 4--20$\\times$ variance reduction is observed for classification supervision on synthetic data but not on human-authored conversations (Section~\\ref{sec:human_data}).\n\nOur contributions:\n\\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*, itemsep=2pt, topsep=4pt]\n  \\item \\textbf{Systematic decomposition of cross-attack generalization.} Controlled ablation across thirteen encoder configurations, three OOD attack families, and two training compositions on synthetic multi-turn data identifies classification-based domain adaptation as the most robust variant; unsupervised pretraining is effective on single-source data but collapses on mixed-LLM training. Three negative results are counter-intuitive: scrambled labels match correct labels (TOST-confirmed on DD; underpowered on ActorAttack), temporal order is irrelevant, and label granularity does not affect detection.\n\n  \\item \\textbf{Scale-dependent observation on domain adaptation.} Three encoder scales (125M, 184M, 355M) suggest adaptation need shrinks with model size, though encoders differ in architecture; same-architecture scaling is needed to isolate scale. RoBERTa-large handles structurally complex attacks without adaptation (Section~\\ref{sec:cross_encoder}).\n\n  \\item \\textbf{Condition-dependent adaptation effects.} A variance reduction of 4--20$\\times$ is observed (3 seeds) on synthetic data, but this benefit reverses on human-authored data. Mixed-LLM training validates that classification-based DAPT remains the strongest variant across training compositions, though the magnitude of advantage varies by attack complexity (Section~\\ref{sec:discussion}). The 9-class taxonomy serves as interpretability tool and ablation infrastructure for label quality assessment (Section~\\ref{sec:strategy}).\n\\end{itemize}\n",

```text
53	
54	Vanilla encoders collapse under cross-attack transfer; domain-adapted variants recover near-perfect detection (Table~\ref{tab:main_results}, Figure~\ref{fig:cross_attack_bar}).
55	DD exhibits high lexical separability (TF-IDF F1\,=\,0.961); ActorAttack, where TF-IDF collapses to 0.261, serves as the primary stress test for beyond-lexical generalization.
56	All methods achieve near-perfect IID F1, reflecting inherent lexical separability when test matches training.
57	On OOD DD, vanilla DeBERTa collapses to F1 = 0.312$\pm$0.026, worsening with more turns (0.628$\pm$0.038 at $K$=1 to 0.312$\pm$0.026 at Full), consistent with the finding that attack turns occupy ``benign'' embedding regions~\citep{bullwinkel2025repeng}.
58	MLM achieves mean F1 $\geq$ 0.97 at all prefixes $K \leq 5$ without any classification labels; domain exposure alone is sufficient.
59	At full conversation length, MLM drops to 0.914$\pm$0.115 (seed 456 recall = 0.64), while the 9-class model maintains 0.994$\pm$0.005.
60	TF-IDF LR reaches F1 = 0.961 on full DD; DD is fully solvable by lexical features.
```

    "oldString": "Three encoders (125M--355M) differ in pretraining; same-architecture scaling is needed. Mean pooling matches BiGRU and shuffling produces $|\\Delta| < 1$\\,pp (Section~\\ref{sec:decomposition}), confirming order-invariant detection. CKA confirms DAPT reshapes representations non-monotonically (Appendix~\\ref{sec:cka_heatmap}). TF-IDF collapse on ActorAttack (0.261 vs.\\ 0.961 on DD) demonstrates beyond-lexical features (Section~\\ref{sec:limitations}).",
    "newString": "Three encoders (125M--355M) differ in pretraining; same-architecture scaling is needed. Mean pooling matches BiGRU and shuffling produces $|\\Delta| < 1$\\,pp (Section~\\ref{sec:decomposition}), confirming order-invariant detection. TF-IDF collapse on ActorAttack (0.261 vs.\\ 0.961 on DD) demonstrates beyond-lexical features (Section~\\ref{sec:limitations}).",
    "originalFile": "% conclusion.tex — compressed for 8-page limit\n\n\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Topic shortcut and label equivalence.}\nThree observations argue against a topic shortcut: unstable topic OOD (F1\\,=\\,0.541$\\pm$0.207), scrambled labels matching correct within 3.9\\,pp, and label-free MLM detection (F1\\,$\\geq$\\,0.97 at $K \\leq 5$). The scrambled-correct equivalence could reflect classification regularizing representations independently of label content, or random permutation over 9 classes preserving enough correlations; distinguishing these requires varying class count. Topic classification occasionally approaches MLM on ActorAttack (0.625 vs.\\ 0.893) despite misaligned labels, reflecting domain exposure rather than the topic objective.\n\n\\paragraph{Single-LLM confound.}\nToxicChat (+18--42\\,pp; Table~\\ref{tab:human_data}) and cross-LLM experiments (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_llm}) confirm transfer. Mixed-LLM 9-class maintains DD F1\\,=\\,0.818$\\pm$0.096 (+53\\,pp) while MLM collapses; one hypothesis is that MLM captures LLM-specific surface patterns while classification extracts style-invariant features. Cross-LLM error decomposition confirms F1 degradation is driven by false positives on unfamiliar benign text, not missed attacks.\n\n\\paragraph{Variance reversal on human data.}\nClassification supervision reduces variance 4--20$\\times$ over MLM on synthetic OOD (3 seeds); on ToxicChat this reverses (std\\,=\\,0.087 vs.\\ 0.013).\nFrozen probing localizes MLM instability to Stage-2: frozen MLM outperforms the full pipeline on ActorAttack (0.941 vs.\\ 0.893), while 9-class frozen matches (0.972; Appendix~\\ref{app:frozen_verify}).\n\n\\paragraph{Encoder capacity, temporal, and lexical analysis.}\nThree encoders (125M--355M) differ in pretraining; same-architecture scaling is needed. Mean pooling matches BiGRU and shuffling produces $|\\Delta| < 1$\\,pp (Section~\\ref{sec:decomposition}), confirming order-invariant detection. CKA confirms DAPT reshapes representations non-monotonically (Appendix~\\ref{sec:cka_heatmap}). TF-IDF collapse on ActorAttack (0.261 vs.\\ 0.961 on DD) demonstrates beyond-lexical features (Section~\\ref{sec:limitations}).\n\n\\section{Conclusion}\n\nClassification-based DAPT is the most robust adaptation for base-size encoders; unsupervised pretraining closes $\\sim$60\\,pp but collapses under mixed-LLM training. Within the architectures tested, larger encoders need less adaptation; 4--20$\\times$ variance reduction reverses on human data. Three negative results---label correctness, temporal order, domain specificity---constrain which properties matter. Continued pretraining is 266$\\times$ faster than LLM-as-judge (Appendix~\\ref{app:efficiency}), offering a practical alternative for multi-turn safety monitoring given matched benign distributions (Appendix~\\ref{app:wildchat_fpr}).\n\n\\section{Limitations}\n\\label{sec:limitations}\n\n\\paragraph{Multi-turn human validation.}\nAll multi-turn findings are derived from synthetic conversations generated by Qwen3-8B. ToxicChat validates transfer to human-authored single-turn attacks (+18--42\\,pp over vanilla), but no human-generated multi-turn jailbreak dataset exists for evaluation. Whether the evidence hierarchy and classification supervision advantages generalize to human-authored multi-turn attacks remains the primary open question.\n\n\\paragraph{Turn-count distribution.}\nBenign conversations contain 12--20 turns, DD jailbreaks 6--10, and ActorAttack jailbreaks 3--8; the three categories have non-overlapping turn counts. Three observations argue against length as the primary detection cue: (a) early detection at equal-length prefixes $K$=1 and $K$=2 achieves AA OOD F1\\,=\\,0.773 and 0.935 (Appendix~\\ref{tab:actorattack_full}); (b) vanilla models access the same length signal but achieve near-zero OOD recall; and (c) truncating benign conversations to 3 turns yields FPR\\,=\\,0\\% (Appendix~\\ref{app:length_independence}).\n\n\\paragraph{Temporal aggregation dilution.}\nWhen subtle attacks (ActorAttack) are followed by extended benign padding, BiGRU temporal aggregation dilutes attack signals (F1 drops from 0.963 to 0.383; Appendix~\\ref{app:length_independence}); in practice, adversaries would not dilute their own successful attacks.\n\n\\paragraph{OOD benign distribution.}\nAll OOD evaluations hold the benign distribution constant (Qwen3-8B generated); results reflect attack-side generalization. On 226 WildChat benign conversations, all variants produce FPR\\,$\\geq$\\,44\\%, with 9-class worst at 68.1\\% (Appendix~\\ref{app:wildchat_fpr}). Even with matched benign distributions, residual calibration error (ECE\\,=\\,0.126--0.141; Appendix~\\ref{app:calibration}) suggests temperature scaling is needed for deployment.\n\n\\paragraph{Frozen encoder assumption.}\nThe frozen Stage-1 design isolates representation quality but may underestimate full-pipeline performance. End-to-end fine-tuning closes this gap to $\\leq$5.8\\,pp on ActorAttack (F1\\,=\\,0.997$\\pm$0.005 vs.\\ 0.939$\\pm$0.047 frozen; Table~\\ref{tab:e2e}; Appendix~\\ref{app:frozen_verify}).\n\n\\paragraph{Dataset scale and cross-LLM transfer.}\nScale validation (1{,}000 conversations; Appendix~\\ref{app:scale_validation}) confirms the hierarchy holds (DAPT advantage 40--56\\,pp over vanilla). Cross-LLM validation shows jailbreak detection transfers when benign distribution is held constant (F1 recovers to 0.800--1.000), but benign-side F1 drops to 0.370--0.430 with unfamiliar benign text (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_llm}). The perturbation evaluation ($n$=76) remains underpowered.\n\n\\paragraph{Strategy labels.}\nPersuasion strategy labels ($n$=50 human validation, $\\kappa$=0.78) are produced by Qwen3-8B; the validation sample yields small per-class sizes across 9 categories, limiting reliability estimates for individual strategies. OOD strategy accuracy degrades to 30.7\\% ($\\kappa$=0.126); interpretability is limited to known attack families. We plan to release annotation prompts and data for independent verification.\n\n\\paragraph{Detection architecture.}\nBinary vs.\\ 9-class equivalence (F1 gap $\\leq$3.9\\,pp) suggests the binary head is not the critical differentiator. FITD provides minimal discriminative signal (all variants F1\\,$\\geq$\\,0.990), serving as confirmation that simple attacks do not challenge any variant.\n\n\\paragraph{Encoder scale.}\nWhether findings hold at LLM-scale encoders (7B+) remains open; same-architecture scaling (e.g., DeBERTa-base vs.\\ DeBERTa-large) is needed to attribute the capacity effect to scale alone.\n\n\\paragraph{Scope and single-turn transfer.}\nOnly persuasion-mediated multi-turn attacks fall within scope; single-turn token-level attacks~\\citep{zou2023gcg}, non-persuasive strategies, and non-text modalities are not addressed. ToxicChat performance reflects zero-shot single-turn transfer; doubling the training corpus drops ToxicChat macro F1 from 0.596 to 0.341$\\pm$0.004 while multi-turn OOD remains stable, confirming the architecture targets multi-turn detection.\n\n\\paragraph{Computation cost.}\nTotal pipeline cost for all seven variants is under 4 GPU-hours on a single A100.\n\n\\section*{Ethics Statement}\n\nThis work is defensive: it improves detection of multi-turn jailbreak attacks rather than developing new attacks. We acknowledge dual-use risk, as the persuasion taxonomy could inform adversarial actors. We mitigate this by using only synthetic conversations (no real harmful content), withholding attack-generation prompts, and focusing contributions on detection mechanisms. No human subjects were involved; all data will be released under CC BY-NC 4.0.\n",
